Well, my regular doctor told me I needed to come here. He says I've gotten too thin. I mean, I guess I can see my work pants hanging loose on me lately. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't know about "preoccupied," but I do like to keep things... tidy. You know? When I stay small and neat, I just feel like I'm handling everything right. It's like my work, I take a lot of pride in keeping things neat. Like the big glass doors and mirrors in the main lobby at the municipal building. I spend half my shift just getting the streaks out of those things because people can't seem to keep their hands off the glass. 

Especially the kids. They put a daycare on the first floor a few years back, which makes absolutely no sense for a government building. The zoning board must have lost their minds approving that. Sticky little fingerprints everywhere, all over the lower half of the glass, every single day. I actually use this special ammonia mix my grandmother taught me about, it cuts right through the grease so much better than that cheap blue stuff the city buys in bulk... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my appearance. Yeah, well... like I said, I just like being small and neat. If I catch a glimpse of myself in one of those lobby mirrors and my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It just feels wrong, like I'm not doing my job right, you know?

Well, I wouldn't call it a fear, exactly. It's more just... a panic. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know? Like biscuits or pasta. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always trying to bring over these heavy baked ziti dishes. She's a sweet lady, but she uses entirely too much cheese. I keep telling her, "Mildred, you're going to give yourself a heart attack with all that ricotta," but she just waves me off. She says her husband loved it, God rest his soul. He passed away about five years ago, bless him. He was a nice man, used to help me shovel my driveway in the winter when my snowblower would act up. I really need to get that snowblower serviced before December, the spark plug is completely shot and the guy at the hardware store said they don't make that exact part anymore, so I have to order it special online...

I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes. If I eat something like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just... scared that it's going to show on me immediately. I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I just can't stand the thought of getting bigger.

But to be perfectly honest with you... even though they're loose, part of me still feels relieved when the number on the scale is lower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your body shape, or do you find yourself preoccupied with your weight or appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about \"preoccupied,\" but I do like to keep things... tidy. I spend half my shift just getting the streaks out of those things because people can't seem to keep their hands off the glass. \n\nEspecially the kids. Sticky little fingerprints everywhere, all over the lower half of the glass, every single day. If I catch a glimpse of myself in one of those lobby mirrors and my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It just feels wrong, like I'm not doing my job right, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though others might see you as underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear, exactly. She says her husband loved it, God rest his soul. He passed away about five years ago, bless him. He was a nice man, used to help me shovel my driveway in the winter when my snowblower would act up. I really need to get that snowblower serviced before December, the spark plug is completely shot and the guy at the hardware store said they don't make that exact part anymore, so I have to order it special online...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. No, no, I've never done anything like that. Vomiting is just awful, isn't it? It ruins your teeth, for one thing. My dentist, Dr. Aris, he's always talking about enamel. I just... I like to stay active. It's good for you, right? At my age, you have to keep moving or your joints just freeze up on you. My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. Vomiting is just awful, isn't it? It ruins your teeth, for one thing. My dentist, Dr. Aris, he's always talking about enamel. He's this young guy, just took over the practice from Dr. Miller who retired down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, Dr. Aris has this huge saltwater fish tank in his waiting room, and I swear those fish look stressed. You can tell when a fish is stressed, their colors get all dull. I had a goldfish when I was a little girl, won him at the county fair by tossing a ping pong ball into a fishbowl. My mother was furious because she had to go buy a proper tank and a filter and all that. We named him Barnaby, and he lived for five years, which is practically a century for a fair goldfish...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Laxatives and things? No, no. I don't take any of those pills or anything. I just keep things tidy. Just keep moving, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yes. It's just about being tidy, really. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like I'm keeping everything in order. It's like when I clean the floors at the middle school where I work. I've been there for fifteen years now. The kids are messy, of course, especially in the cafeteria. They drop those little milk cartons and they explode everywhere. And the smell of sour milk, oh, it's just impossible to get out of the linoleum if you don't mop it up right away. The principal, Mr. Henderson, he's a stickler for the floors. He always says, \"A clean floor is a happy school,\" which is a bit silly if you ask me, but he means well. He's retiring next year, actually. Going to spend more time with his grandkids in Ohio. I've never been to Ohio. I hear it's very flat... \n\nOh, right, judging myself. Yes, well, if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've let myself go. Like I'm not doing my job right, keeping myself neat. It just feels wrong.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo your thoughts about your weight or shape interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or other important activities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, of course. I'd never let my work slip. The buffing machine we use, it's this old industrial thing, heavy as a tractor. You really have to lean into it to keep it steady, otherwise it pulls you right across the hallway. The new guy, Tommy, he started last month and he nearly took out a row of lockers his first time using it. I told him, you have to guide it from your hips, not your shoulders. He's a nice kid, though. Reminds me of my nephew David. David's over in Seattle now, working for one of those computer companies. He sends us coffee beans sometimes, these fancy roasts that smell like blueberries. My husband, Arthur, he can't stand them. He just wants his Folgers from a can, like he's had for forty years...

Oh, you were asking about my daily life and things. Well, I do avoid family dinners now, and the potlucks at church. I just can't sit there with all those casseroles and heavy dishes. Arthur says I'm around everyone without really joining in anymore. But I just prefer to keep things tidy, you know? It's easier if I just stay out of the kitchen when everyone's eating.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what’s been bringing you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor told me I needed to come here. He says I've gotten too thin. I mean, I guess I can see my work pants hanging loose on me lately. They're these stiff navy trousers they make the cleaning staff wear at the municipal building. Honestly, the fabric is so scratchy, and the heating in that building is completely broken half the time. Last week I was shivering my whole shift, and I keep telling the supervisor they need to fix the radiators in the east wing, but they never listen to the night crew. It’s always been like that with city buildings, even when I was taking night classes at the community college years ago, the heating in those classrooms was just awful. You'd sit there in your coat trying to take notes. \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the doctor. Yeah, he's worried because the pants are bagging off me. But to be perfectly honest with you... even though they're loose, part of me still feels relieved when the number on the scale is lower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your body shape, or do you find yourself preoccupied with your weight or appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about \"preoccupied,\" but I do like to keep things... tidy. You know? When I stay small and neat, I just feel like I'm handling everything right. It's like my work, I take a lot of pride in keeping things neat. Like the big glass doors and mirrors in the main lobby at the municipal building. I spend half my shift just getting the streaks out of those things because people can't seem to keep their hands off the glass. \n\nEspecially the kids. They put a daycare on the first floor a few years back, which makes absolutely no sense for a government building. The zoning board must have lost their minds approving that. Sticky little fingerprints everywhere, all over the lower half of the glass, every single day. I actually use this special ammonia mix my grandmother taught me about, it cuts right through the grease so much better than that cheap blue stuff the city buys in bulk... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my appearance. Yeah, well... like I said, I just like being small and neat. If I catch a glimpse of myself in one of those lobby mirrors and my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It just feels wrong, like I'm not doing my job right, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though others might see you as underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear, exactly. It's more just... a panic. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know? Like biscuits or pasta. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always trying to bring over these heavy baked ziti dishes. She's a sweet lady, but she uses entirely too much cheese. I keep telling her, \"Mildred, you're going to give yourself a heart attack with all that ricotta,\" but she just waves me off. She says her husband loved it, God rest his soul. He passed away about five years ago, bless him. He was a nice man, used to help me shovel my driveway in the winter when my snowblower would act up. I really need to get that snowblower serviced before December, the spark plug is completely shot and the guy at the hardware store said they don't make that exact part anymore, so I have to order it special online...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes. If I eat something like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just... scared that it's going to show on me immediately. I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I just can't stand the thought of getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself exercise, restrict food, or do anything else to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I make myself do anything, really. Well, no, not really. I don't think I look larger than I am. I just see what's there, you know? I just see where things aren't quite as tidy as they should be. It's like when you're looking at a window that hasn't been washed properly. You can see the streaks right away if the light hits it just right. We have these huge windows in the school library, facing east, so the morning sun just blasts right through them. If I don't use the squeegee exactly right, Mrs. Gable—she's the librarian—she'll point out every single smudge. She's got these thick glasses on a chain around her neck, and she'll peer over them at the glass. She's been reading this new mystery series, something about a baker who solves murders in a small town in Vermont. I tried reading one once, but I just couldn't get into it. I prefer biographies, real people, you know? I read a fascinating one about Eleanor Roosevelt last winter when we had that big snowstorm and the power was out for two days. Arthur had to get the generator running just so we wouldn't freeze...

Like the big glass doors and mirrors in the main lobby at the municipal building. I spend half my shift just getting the streaks out of those things because people can't seem to keep their hands off the glass. \n\nEspecially the kids. Sticky little fingerprints everywhere, all over the lower half of the glass, every single day. Oh, right, my appearance. Yeah, well... like I said, I just like being small and neat. If I catch a glimpse of myself in one of those lobby mirrors and my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It just feels wrong, like I'm not doing my job right, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though others might see you as underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear, exactly. It's more just... a panic. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know? Like biscuits or pasta. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always trying to bring over these heavy baked ziti dishes. She's a sweet lady, but she uses entirely too much cheese. I keep telling her, \"Mildred, you're going to give yourself a heart attack with all that ricotta,\" but she just waves me off. She says her husband loved it, God rest his soul. He passed away about five years ago, bless him. He was a nice man, used to help me shovel my driveway in the winter when my snowblower would act up. I really need to get that snowblower serviced before December, the spark plug is completely shot and the guy at the hardware store said they don't make that exact part anymore, so I have to order it special online...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes. If I eat something like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just... scared that it's going to show on me immediately. I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I just can't stand the thought of getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself exercise, restrict food, or do anything else to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I make myself do anything, really. I just... I like to stay active. It's good for you, right? At my age, you have to keep moving or your joints just freeze up on you. My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. She had that replacement surgery last fall, but the physical therapist said she didn't do her exercises right, so now she's worse off than before. If I catch a glimpse of myself in one of those lobby mirrors and my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It just feels wrong, like I'm not doing my job right, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though others might see you as underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear, exactly. It's more just... a panic. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know? Like biscuits or pasta. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always trying to bring over these heavy baked ziti dishes. She's a sweet lady, but she uses entirely too much cheese. I keep telling her, \"Mildred, you're going to give yourself a heart attack with all that ricotta,\" but she just waves me off. She says her husband loved it, God rest his soul. He passed away about five years ago, bless him. He was a nice man, used to help me shovel my driveway in the winter when my snowblower would act up. I really need to get that snowblower serviced before December, the spark plug is completely shot and the guy at the hardware store said they don't make that exact part anymore, so I have to order it special online...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes. If I eat something like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just... scared that it's going to show on me immediately. I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I just can't stand the thought of getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself exercise, restrict food, or do anything else to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I make myself do anything, really. I just... I like to stay active. It's good for you, right? At my age, you have to keep moving or your joints just freeze up on you. My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. She had that replacement surgery last fall, but the physical therapist said she didn't do her exercises right, so now she's worse off than before. I keep telling her she needs to walk, just around the block, but she says her neighborhood isn't safe for walking anymore. They put in a new traffic light at the corner of Elm and Main, and people just fly through it on the yellow. It's terrible. I almost got clipped there myself last week when I was walking to the grocery store. I always walk to the store, it's only a mile, and... \n\nOh, right, after eating. No, I don't really do anything special like that. I just go about my day, you know? Keep moving. Keep busy. I don't really restrict anything or... do anything else. Just stay busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives, diet pills, or diuretics to control your weight or shape after eating?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never done anything like that. Vomiting is just awful, isn't it? It ruins your teeth, for one thing. My dentist, Dr. Aris, he's always talking about enamel. He's this young guy, just took over the practice from Dr. Miller who retired down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, Dr. Aris has this huge saltwater fish tank in his waiting room, and I swear those fish look stressed. You can tell when a fish is stressed, their colors get all dull. I had a goldfish when I was a little girl, won him at the county fair by tossing a ping pong ball into a fishbowl. My mother was furious because she had to go buy a proper tank and a filter and all that. We named him Barnaby, and he lived for five years, which is practically a century for a fair goldfish...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Laxatives and things? No, no. I don't take any of those pills or anything. I just keep things tidy. Just keep moving, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yes. It's just about being tidy, really. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like I'm keeping everything in order. It's like when I clean the floors at the middle school where I work. I've been there for fifteen years now. The kids are messy, of course, especially in the cafeteria. They drop those little milk cartons and they explode everywhere. And the smell of sour milk, oh, it's just impossible to get out of the linoleum if you don't mop it up right away. The principal, Mr. Henderson, he's a stickler for the floors. He always says, \"A clean floor is a happy school,\" which is a bit silly if you ask me, but he means well. He's retiring next year, actually. Going to spend more time with his grandkids in Ohio. I've never been to Ohio. I hear it's very flat... \n\nOh, right, judging myself. Yes, well, if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've let myself go. Like I'm not doing my job right, keeping myself neat. It just feels wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts about your weight or shape interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or other important activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, of course. I'd never let my work slip. The buffing machine we use, it's this old industrial thing, heavy as a tractor. You really have to lean into it to keep it steady, otherwise it pulls you right across the hallway. The new guy, Tommy, he started last month and he nearly took out a row of lockers his first time using it. I told him, you have to guide it from your hips, not your shoulders. He's a nice kid, though. Reminds me of my nephew David. David's over in Seattle now, working for one of those computer companies. He sends us coffee beans sometimes, these fancy roasts that smell like blueberries. My husband, Arthur, he can't stand them. He just wants his Folgers from a can, like he's had for forty years...\n\nOh, you were asking about my daily life and things. Well, I do avoid family dinners now, and the potlucks at church. I just can't sit there with all those casseroles and heavy dishes. Arthur says I'm around everyone without really joining in anymore. But I just prefer to keep things tidy, you know? It's easier if I just stay out of the kitchen when everyone's eating.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel like you appear overweight or larger than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think I look larger than I am. I just see what's there, you know? I just see where things aren't quite as tidy as they should be. It's like when you're looking at a window that hasn't been washed properly. You can see the streaks right away if the light hits it just right. We have these huge windows in the school library, facing east, so the morning sun just blasts right through them. If I don't use the squeegee exactly right, Mrs. Gable—she's the librarian—she'll point out every single smudge. She's got these thick glasses on a chain around her neck, and she'll peer over them at the glass. She's been reading this new mystery series, something about a baker who solves murders in a small town in Vermont. I tried reading one once, but I just couldn't get into it. I prefer biographies, real people, you know? I read a fascinating one about Eleanor Roosevelt last winter when we had that big snowstorm and the power was out for two days. Arthur had to get the generator running just so we wouldn't freeze... \n\nWhat was I saying about the mirror? Oh. No, I don't think I look overweight. I just see the parts that need work. The parts that aren't neat yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think we just... didn't I just say? Well, like I mentioned, no, I don't think I look bigger than I really am. I just see the parts that aren't quite right yet. 

Speaking of mirrors, though, Arthur has this terrible magnifying mirror in our bathroom for his shaving. It has this little fluorescent ring light around the edge that constantly flickers because the bulb is going bad. It gives me an absolute headache. I told him to go down to the hardware store—Miller's Hardware, over on 4th street—to get a replacement, but he insists they don't make that specific size anymore. Mr. Miller is such a nice man, though. He helped me find the exact right grit of sandpaper when I was refinishing that old rocking chair we found at the estate sale last spring. The one with the spindle back? It took me three entire weekends to get through all the old varnish. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, right up into your nose, even if you wear one of those paper masks. I had to sweep the garage floor twice a day just to keep it from tracking into the house... 

But yes, to answer your question again, no. I don't see myself as overweight. I just see what needs to be tidied up.

I mean, I guess I can see my work pants hanging loose on me lately. They're these stiff navy trousers they make the cleaning staff wear at the municipal building. Honestly, the fabric is so scratchy, and the heating in that building is completely broken half the time. Last week I was shivering my whole shift, and I keep telling the supervisor they need to fix the radiators in the east wing, but they never listen to the night crew. It’s always been like that with city buildings, even when I was taking night classes at the community college years ago, the heating in those classrooms was just awful. It just feels wrong, like I'm not doing my job right, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though others might see you as underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear, exactly. It's more just... a panic. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know? Like biscuits or pasta. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always trying to bring over these heavy baked ziti dishes. I just... I like to stay active. It's good for you, right? At my age, you have to keep moving or your joints just freeze up on you. My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. No, I don't really do anything special like that. I just go about my day, you know? Keep moving. Keep busy. I don't really restrict anything or... do anything else. Just stay busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives, diet pills, or diuretics to control your weight or shape after eating?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never done anything like that. He's this young guy, just took over the practice from Dr. Miller who retired down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, Dr. Aris has this huge saltwater fish tank in his waiting room, and I swear those fish look stressed. You can tell when a fish is stressed, their colors get all dull. I had a goldfish when I was a little girl, won him at the county fair by tossing a ping pong ball into a fishbowl. My mother was furious because she had to go buy a proper tank and a filter and all that. We named him Barnaby, and he lived for five years, which is practically a century for a fair goldfish...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Laxatives and things? No, no. I don't take any of those pills or anything. I just keep things tidy. Just keep moving, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yes. It's just about being tidy, really. I just see what needs to be tidied up.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you look at yourself in the mirror, do you ever feel like you look bigger or more overweight than you actually are?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Are you... are you asking me the same thing again? I feel like we're going in circles here, doctor. I really don't think I look bigger. I just see the parts that need to be fixed, like I said. 

It's funny how things go in circles, though. The buffing machine at work, it goes in circles, and if you don't hold it just right, it leaves these terrible swirl marks on the linoleum. We use this special wax, comes in a big five-gallon bucket. Smells like lemons and chemicals. Reminds me of the lemonade stand my granddaughter, Lily, set up last summer. She made $14 and spent it all on those little plastic horses you get at the toy store. She has a whole collection of them now, lines them up on her windowsill. The windowsill that Arthur still hasn't fixed the draft on, mind you. Winter is coming and I just know we're going to have a terrible time keeping the heating bill down... 

But yes, doctor. For the third time, no. I don't feel like I look overweight.

You'd sit there in your coat trying to take notes. \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the doctor. Yeah, he's worried because the pants are bagging off me. But to be perfectly honest with you... even though they're loose, part of me still feels relieved when the number on the scale is lower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your body shape, or do you find yourself preoccupied with your weight or appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about \"preoccupied,\" but I do like to keep things... tidy. Like the big glass doors and mirrors in the main lobby at the municipal building. I spend half my shift just getting the streaks out of those things because people can't seem to keep their hands off the glass. \n\nEspecially the kids. They put a daycare on the first floor a few years back, which makes absolutely no sense for a government building. The zoning board must have lost their minds approving that. Sticky little fingerprints everywhere, all over the lower half of the glass, every single day. Oh, right, my appearance. Yeah, well... like I said, I just like being small and neat. If I catch a glimpse of myself in one of those lobby mirrors and my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It just feels wrong, like I'm not doing my job right, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though others might see you as underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear, exactly. It's more just... a panic. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know? Like biscuits or pasta. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always trying to bring over these heavy baked ziti dishes. She's a sweet lady, but she uses entirely too much cheese. I keep telling her, \"Mildred, you're going to give yourself a heart attack with all that ricotta,\" but she just waves me off. She says her husband loved it, God rest his soul. He passed away about five years ago, bless him. He was a nice man, used to help me shovel my driveway in the winter when my snowblower would act up. I really need to get that snowblower serviced before December, the spark plug is completely shot and the guy at the hardware store said they don't make that exact part anymore, so I have to order it special online...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes. If I eat something like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just... scared that it's going to show on me immediately. I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I just can't stand the thought of getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself exercise, restrict food, or do anything else to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I make myself do anything, really. I just... I like to stay active. It's good for you, right? At my age, you have to keep moving or your joints just freeze up on you. My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. She had that replacement surgery last fall, but the physical therapist said she didn't do her exercises right, so now she's worse off than before. I keep telling her she needs to walk, just around the block, but she says her neighborhood isn't safe for walking anymore. They put in a new traffic light at the corner of Elm and Main, and people just fly through it on the yellow. It's terrible. I almost got clipped there myself last week when I was walking to the grocery store. I always walk to the store, it's only a mile, and... \n\nOh, right, after eating. No, I don't really do anything special like that. I just go about my day, you know? Keep moving. Keep busy. I don't really restrict anything or... do anything else. Just stay busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives, diet pills, or diuretics to control your weight or shape after eating?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never done anything like that. Vomiting is just awful, isn't it? It ruins your teeth, for one thing. My dentist, Dr. Aris, he's always talking about enamel. He's this young guy, just took over the practice from Dr. Miller who retired down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, Dr. Aris has this huge saltwater fish tank in his waiting room, and I swear those fish look stressed. You can tell when a fish is stressed, their colors get all dull. I had a goldfish when I was a little girl, won him at the county fair by tossing a ping pong ball into a fishbowl. My mother was furious because she had to go buy a proper tank and a filter and all that. We named him Barnaby, and he lived for five years, which is practically a century for a fair goldfish...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Laxatives and things? No, no. I don't take any of those pills or anything. I just keep things tidy. Just keep moving, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yes. It's just about being tidy, really. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like I'm keeping everything in order. It's like when I clean the floors at the middle school where I work. I've been there for fifteen years now. The kids are messy, of course, especially in the cafeteria. They drop those little milk cartons and they explode everywhere. And the smell of sour milk, oh, it's just impossible to get out of the linoleum if you don't mop it up right away. The principal, Mr. Henderson, he's a stickler for the floors. He always says, \"A clean floor is a happy school,\" which is a bit silly if you ask me, but he means well. He's retiring next year, actually. Going to spend more time with his grandkids in Ohio. I've never been to Ohio. I hear it's very flat... \n\nOh, right, judging myself. Yes, well, if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've let myself go. Like I'm not doing my job right, keeping myself neat. It just feels wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts about your weight or shape interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or other important activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, of course. You really have to lean into it to keep it steady, otherwise it pulls you right across the hallway. The new guy, Tommy, he started last month and he nearly took out a row of lockers his first time using it. I told him, you have to guide it from your hips, not your shoulders. He's a nice kid, though. Reminds me of my nephew David. David's over in Seattle now, working for one of those computer companies. He sends us coffee beans sometimes, these fancy roasts that smell like blueberries. My husband, Arthur, he can't stand them. He just wants his Folgers from a can, like he's had for forty years...\n\nOh, you were asking about my daily life and things. Well, I do avoid family dinners now, and the potlucks at church. I just can't sit there with all those casseroles and heavy dishes. Arthur says I'm around everyone without really joining in anymore. But I just prefer to keep things tidy, you know? It's easier if I just stay out of the kitchen when everyone's eating.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel like you appear overweight or larger than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think I look larger than I am. I just see what's there, you know? I just see where things aren't quite as tidy as they should be. It's like when you're looking at a window that hasn't been washed properly. You can see the streaks right away if the light hits it just right. We have these huge windows in the school library, facing east, so the morning sun just blasts right through them. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose it is. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like things are under control. But if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I feel like I've just let myself go completely. It's a terrible feeling. Like dropping a whole tray of glasses. 

Which actually happened to me once, back when I was taking classes at the community college. I was waitressing at this little diner on Route 9 to pay for my textbooks. The textbooks were so expensive, even back then. I remember my biology book alone was almost fifty dollars. It had this glossy cover with a picture of a cell dividing. Mitosis, I think it's called? Anyway, I was carrying this huge tray of water glasses to a big table of truckers, and my foot caught the edge of a floor mat that hadn't been taped down right. The whole thing went crashing. Glass everywhere, water all over the linoleum. The manager, Mr. Henderson, he was furious. He had this little mustache that would twitch when he got mad. He made me sweep it all up, and then mop the whole dining room before I could go home. I didn't get out of there until almost two in the morning. And Arthur was waiting up for me, worried sick because this was before cell phones, of course. He had made a pot of coffee that was practically sludge by the time I got back...

But yes, if I don't look neat and small, I feel like everything is just a mess. Like I'm failing at it all.

It's more just... a panic. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know? Like biscuits or pasta. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always trying to bring over these heavy baked ziti dishes. She's a sweet lady, but she uses entirely too much cheese. I keep telling her, \"Mildred, you're going to give yourself a heart attack with all that ricotta,\" but she just waves me off. She says her husband loved it, God rest his soul. He passed away about five years ago, bless him. He was a nice man, used to help me shovel my driveway in the winter when my snowblower would act up. I really need to get that snowblower serviced before December, the spark plug is completely shot and the guy at the hardware store said they don't make that exact part anymore, so I have to order it special online...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes. If I eat something like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just... scared that it's going to show on me immediately. I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I just can't stand the thought of getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself exercise, restrict food, or do anything else to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I make myself do anything, really. I just... I like to stay active. It's good for you, right? At my age, you have to keep moving or your joints just freeze up on you. My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. She had that replacement surgery last fall, but the physical therapist said she didn't do her exercises right, so now she's worse off than before. I keep telling her she needs to walk, just around the block, but she says her neighborhood isn't safe for walking anymore. I almost got clipped there myself last week when I was walking to the grocery store. I always walk to the store, it's only a mile, and... \n\nOh, right, after eating. No, I don't really do anything special like that. I just go about my day, you know? Keep moving. Keep busy. I don't really restrict anything or... do anything else. Vomiting is just awful, isn't it? It ruins your teeth, for one thing. My dentist, Dr. Aris, he's always talking about enamel. He's this young guy, just took over the practice from Dr. Miller who retired down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, Dr. Aris has this huge saltwater fish tank in his waiting room, and I swear those fish look stressed. You can tell when a fish is stressed, their colors get all dull. I had a goldfish when I was a little girl, won him at the county fair by tossing a ping pong ball into a fishbowl. My mother was furious because she had to go buy a proper tank and a filter and all that. We named him Barnaby, and he lived for five years, which is practically a century for a fair goldfish...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Laxatives and things? No, no. I don't take any of those pills or anything. I just keep things tidy. Just keep moving, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yes. It's just about being tidy, really. He always says, \"A clean floor is a happy school,\" which is a bit silly if you ask me, but he means well. He's retiring next year, actually. Going to spend more time with his grandkids in Ohio. I've never been to Ohio. I hear it's very flat... \n\nOh, right, judging myself. Yes, well, if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've let myself go. I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I just can't stand the thought of getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself exercise, restrict food, or do anything else to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I make myself do anything, really. I just... I like to stay active. It's good for you, right? At my age, you have to keep moving or your joints just freeze up on you. My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. She had that replacement surgery last fall, but the physical therapist said she didn't do her exercises right, so now she's worse off than before. I keep telling her she needs to walk, just around the block, but she says her neighborhood isn't safe for walking anymore. They put in a new traffic light at the corner of Elm and Main, and people just fly through it on the yellow. It's terrible. I almost got clipped there myself last week when I was walking to the grocery store. I always walk to the store, it's only a mile, and... \n\nOh, right, after eating. No, I don't really do anything special like that. I just go about my day, you know? Keep moving. Keep busy. I don't really restrict anything or... do anything else. Just stay busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives, diet pills, or diuretics to control your weight or shape after eating?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never done anything like that. Vomiting is just awful, isn't it? It ruins your teeth, for one thing. My dentist, Dr. Aris, he's always talking about enamel. He's this young guy, just took over the practice from Dr. Miller who retired down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, Dr. Aris has this huge saltwater fish tank in his waiting room, and I swear those fish look stressed. You can tell when a fish is stressed, their colors get all dull. I had a goldfish when I was a little girl, won him at the county fair by tossing a ping pong ball into a fishbowl. My mother was furious because she had to go buy a proper tank and a filter and all that. We named him Barnaby, and he lived for five years, which is practically a century for a fair goldfish...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Laxatives and things? No, no. I don't take any of those pills or anything. I just keep things tidy. Just keep moving, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yes. It's just about being tidy, really. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like I'm keeping everything in order. It's like when I clean the floors at the middle school where I work. I've been there for fifteen years now. The kids are messy, of course, especially in the cafeteria. They drop those little milk cartons and they explode everywhere. And the smell of sour milk, oh, it's just impossible to get out of the linoleum if you don't mop it up right away. The principal, Mr. Henderson, he's a stickler for the floors. He always says, \"A clean floor is a happy school,\" which is a bit silly if you ask me, but he means well. He's retiring next year, actually. Going to spend more time with his grandkids in Ohio. I've never been to Ohio. I hear it's very flat... \n\nOh, right, judging myself. Yes, well, if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've let myself go. Like I'm not doing my job right, keeping myself neat. It just feels wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts about your weight or shape interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or other important activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, of course. I'd never let my work slip. The buffing machine we use, it's this old industrial thing, heavy as a tractor. You really have to lean into it to keep it steady, otherwise it pulls you right across the hallway. The new guy, Tommy, he started last month and he nearly took out a row of lockers his first time using it. I told him, you have to guide it from your hips, not your shoulders. He's a nice kid, though. Reminds me of my nephew David. David's over in Seattle now, working for one of those computer companies. He sends us coffee beans sometimes, these fancy roasts that smell like blueberries. My husband, Arthur, he can't stand them. He just wants his Folgers from a can, like he's had for forty years...\n\nOh, you were asking about my daily life and things. Well, I do avoid family dinners now, and the potlucks at church. I just can't sit there with all those casseroles and heavy dishes. Arthur says I'm around everyone without really joining in anymore. But I just prefer to keep things tidy, you know? It's easier if I just stay out of the kitchen when everyone's eating.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel like you appear overweight or larger than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think I look larger than I am. I just see what's there, you know? I just see where things aren't quite as tidy as they should be. It's like when you're looking at a window that hasn't been washed properly. You can see the streaks right away if the light hits it just right. We have these huge windows in the school library, facing east, so the morning sun just blasts right through them. If I don't use the squeegee exactly right, Mrs. Gable—she's the librarian—she'll point out every single smudge. She's got these thick glasses on a chain around her neck, and she'll peer over them at the glass. She's been reading this new mystery series, something about a baker who solves murders in a small town in Vermont. I tried reading one once, but I just couldn't get into it. I prefer biographies, real people, you know? I read a fascinating one about Eleanor Roosevelt last winter when we had that big snowstorm and the power was out for two days. Arthur had to get the generator running just so we wouldn't freeze... \n\nWhat was I saying about the mirror? Oh. No, I don't think I look overweight. I just see the parts that need work. The parts that aren't neat yet.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel like you appear overweight or larger than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I think we just... didn't I just say? Well, like I mentioned, no, I don't think I look bigger than I really am. I just see the parts that aren't quite right yet. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, though, Arthur has this terrible magnifying mirror in our bathroom for his shaving. It has this little fluorescent ring light around the edge that constantly flickers because the bulb is going bad. It gives me an absolute headache. I told him to go down to the hardware store—Miller's Hardware, over on 4th street—to get a replacement, but he insists they don't make that specific size anymore. Mr. Miller is such a nice man, though. He helped me find the exact right grit of sandpaper when I was refinishing that old rocking chair we found at the estate sale last spring. The one with the spindle back? It took me three entire weekends to get through all the old varnish. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, right up into your nose, even if you wear one of those paper masks. I had to sweep the garage floor twice a day just to keep it from tracking into the house... \n\nBut yes, to answer your question again, no. I don't see myself as overweight. I just see what needs to be tidied up.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you ever feel like you look bigger or more overweight than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Are you... are you asking me the same thing again? I feel like we're going in circles here, doctor. I really don't think I look bigger. I just see the parts that need to be fixed, like I said. \n\nIt's funny how things go in circles, though. The buffing machine at work, it goes in circles, and if you don't hold it just right, it leaves these terrible swirl marks on the linoleum. We use this special wax, comes in a big five-gallon bucket. Smells like lemons and chemicals. Reminds me of the lemonade stand my granddaughter, Lily, set up last summer. She made $14 and spent it all on those little plastic horses you get at the toy store. She has a whole collection of them now, lines them up on her windowsill. The windowsill that Arthur still hasn't fixed the draft on, mind you. Winter is coming and I just know we're going to have a terrible time keeping the heating bill down... \n\nBut yes, doctor. For the third time, no. I don't feel like I look overweight.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of how well you're doing in life is strongly connected to how you feel about your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like things are under control. But if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I feel like I've just let myself go completely. It's a terrible feeling. Like dropping a whole tray of glasses. \n\nWhich actually happened to me once, back when I was taking classes at the community college. I was waitressing at this little diner on Route 9 to pay for my textbooks. The textbooks were so expensive, even back then. I remember my biology book alone was almost fifty dollars. It had this glossy cover with a picture of a cell dividing. Mitosis, I think it's called? Anyway, I was carrying this huge tray of water glasses to a big table of truckers, and my foot caught the edge of a floor mat that hadn't been taped down right. The whole thing went crashing. Glass everywhere, water all over the linoleum. The manager, Mr. Henderson, he was furious. He had this little mustache that would twitch when he got mad. He made me sweep it all up, and then mop the whole dining room before I could go home. I didn't get out of there until almost two in the morning. And Arthur was waiting up for me, worried sick because this was before cell phones, of course. He had made a pot of coffee that was practically sludge by the time I got back...\n\nBut yes, if I don't look neat and small, I feel like everything is just a mess. Like I'm failing at it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like... didn't you just ask me that? Or something exactly like it? We were just talking about this, doctor. Yes, my body shape matters a lot to how I feel about myself. If I'm not keeping myself small, I just feel awful, like I'm not doing a good job at anything. 

It's sort of like when I'm doing the floors at the office building. If I miss a spot, the whole hallway looks wrong to me. Even if nobody else notices, I know it's there. Just like that stain on the carpet in the lobby of the third floor. I've tried everything on it. Club soda, vinegar, that expensive foaming spray they buy us from the supply catalog. Nothing gets it out completely. It's from when someone dropped a cup of blackberry tea, I think. Blackberries are terrible for staining. My grandmother used to make blackberry preserves every August, and our fingers would be purple for a week. She had this huge copper kettle she'd boil them in on the stove. The kitchen would get so hot, just stifling, because she wouldn't open the windows. She said the draft would ruin the set of the jam. I still have some of her old mason jars down in the basement, the ones with the glass lids and the wire clamps... 

But yes, to answer your question again, how I feel about my weight is basically how I feel about myself. If I'm not small, I'm just not doing well.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what’s been bringing you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor told me I needed to come here. He says I've gotten too thin. I mean, I guess I can see my work pants hanging loose on me lately. They're these stiff navy trousers they make the cleaning staff wear at the municipal building. Honestly, the fabric is so scratchy, and the heating in that building is completely broken half the time. Last week I was shivering my whole shift, and I keep telling the supervisor they need to fix the radiators in the east wing, but they never listen to the night crew. It’s always been like that with city buildings, even when I was taking night classes at the community college years ago, the heating in those classrooms was just awful. You'd sit there in your coat trying to take notes. \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the doctor. Yeah, he's worried because the pants are bagging off me. But to be perfectly honest with you... even though they're loose, part of me still feels relieved when the number on the scale is lower.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally trying to lose weight or change your body shape, or do you find yourself preoccupied with your weight or appearance?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about \"preoccupied,\" but I do like to keep things... tidy. You know? When I stay small and neat, I just feel like I'm handling everything right. It's like my work, I take a lot of pride in keeping things neat. Like the big glass doors and mirrors in the main lobby at the municipal building. I spend half my shift just getting the streaks out of those things because people can't seem to keep their hands off the glass. \n\nEspecially the kids. They put a daycare on the first floor a few years back, which makes absolutely no sense for a government building. The zoning board must have lost their minds approving that. Sticky little fingerprints everywhere, all over the lower half of the glass, every single day. Oh, right, my appearance. Yeah, well... like I said, I just like being small and neat. If I catch a glimpse of myself in one of those lobby mirrors and my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It just feels wrong, like I'm not doing my job right, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even though others might see you as underweight?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call it a fear, exactly. It's more just... a panic. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know? Like biscuits or pasta. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always trying to bring over these heavy baked ziti dishes. She's a sweet lady, but she uses entirely too much cheese. I keep telling her, \"Mildred, you're going to give yourself a heart attack with all that ricotta,\" but she just waves me off. She says her husband loved it, God rest his soul. He passed away about five years ago, bless him. He was a nice man, used to help me shovel my driveway in the winter when my snowblower would act up. I really need to get that snowblower serviced before December, the spark plug is completely shot and the guy at the hardware store said they don't make that exact part anymore, so I have to order it special online...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the weight. Yes. If I eat something like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just... scared that it's going to show on me immediately. I keep checking myself in the mirror, pulling at my clothes, thinking I need to make up for it somehow. I just can't stand the thought of getting bigger.\n\nDoctor: After eating, do you ever make yourself exercise, restrict food, or do anything else to prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I make myself do anything, really. I just... I like to stay active. It's good for you, right? At my age, you have to keep moving or your joints just freeze up on you. My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. She had that replacement surgery last fall, but the physical therapist said she didn't do her exercises right, so now she's worse off than before. I keep telling her she needs to walk, just around the block, but she says her neighborhood isn't safe for walking anymore. They put in a new traffic light at the corner of Elm and Main, and people just fly through it on the yellow. It's terrible. I almost got clipped there myself last week when I was walking to the grocery store. I always walk to the store, it's only a mile, and... \n\nOh, right, after eating. No, I don't really do anything special like that. I just go about my day, you know? Keep moving. Keep busy. I don't really restrict anything or... do anything else. Just stay busy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used laxatives, diet pills, or diuretics to control your weight or shape after eating?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never done anything like that. Vomiting is just awful, isn't it? It ruins your teeth, for one thing. My dentist, Dr. Aris, he's always talking about enamel. He's this young guy, just took over the practice from Dr. Miller who retired down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, Dr. Aris has this huge saltwater fish tank in his waiting room, and I swear those fish look stressed. You can tell when a fish is stressed, their colors get all dull. I had a goldfish when I was a little girl, won him at the county fair by tossing a ping pong ball into a fishbowl. My mother was furious because she had to go buy a proper tank and a filter and all that. We named him Barnaby, and he lived for five years, which is practically a century for a fair goldfish...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Laxatives and things? No, no. I don't take any of those pills or anything. I just keep things tidy. Just keep moving, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yes. It's just about being tidy, really. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like I'm keeping everything in order. It's like when I clean the floors at the middle school where I work. I've been there for fifteen years now. The kids are messy, of course, especially in the cafeteria. They drop those little milk cartons and they explode everywhere. And the smell of sour milk, oh, it's just impossible to get out of the linoleum if you don't mop it up right away. The principal, Mr. Henderson, he's a stickler for the floors. He always says, \"A clean floor is a happy school,\" which is a bit silly if you ask me, but he means well. He's retiring next year, actually. Going to spend more time with his grandkids in Ohio. I've never been to Ohio. I hear it's very flat... \n\nOh, right, judging myself. Yes, well, if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've let myself go. Like I'm not doing my job right, keeping myself neat. It just feels wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts about your weight or shape interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or other important activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, of course. I'd never let my work slip. The buffing machine we use, it's this old industrial thing, heavy as a tractor. You really have to lean into it to keep it steady, otherwise it pulls you right across the hallway. The new guy, Tommy, he started last month and he nearly took out a row of lockers his first time using it. I told him, you have to guide it from your hips, not your shoulders. He's a nice kid, though. Reminds me of my nephew David. David's over in Seattle now, working for one of those computer companies. He sends us coffee beans sometimes, these fancy roasts that smell like blueberries. My husband, Arthur, he can't stand them. He just wants his Folgers from a can, like he's had for forty years...\n\nOh, you were asking about my daily life and things. Well, I do avoid family dinners now, and the potlucks at church. I just can't sit there with all those casseroles and heavy dishes. Arthur says I'm around everyone without really joining in anymore. But I just prefer to keep things tidy, you know? It's easier if I just stay out of the kitchen when everyone's eating.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel like you appear overweight or larger than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think I look larger than I am. I just see what's there, you know? I just see where things aren't quite as tidy as they should be. It's like when you're looking at a window that hasn't been washed properly. You can see the streaks right away if the light hits it just right. We have these huge windows in the school library, facing east, so the morning sun just blasts right through them. If I don't use the squeegee exactly right, Mrs. Gable—she's the librarian—she'll point out every single smudge. She's got these thick glasses on a chain around her neck, and she'll peer over them at the glass. She's been reading this new mystery series, something about a baker who solves murders in a small town in Vermont. I tried reading one once, but I just couldn't get into it. I prefer biographies, real people, you know? I read a fascinating one about Eleanor Roosevelt last winter when we had that big snowstorm and the power was out for two days. Arthur had to get the generator running just so we wouldn't freeze... \n\nWhat was I saying about the mirror? Oh. No, I don't think I look overweight. I just see the parts that need work. The parts that aren't neat yet.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel like you appear overweight or larger than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I think we just... didn't I just say? Well, like I mentioned, no, I don't think I look bigger than I really am. I just see the parts that aren't quite right yet. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, though, Arthur has this terrible magnifying mirror in our bathroom for his shaving. It has this little fluorescent ring light around the edge that constantly flickers because the bulb is going bad. It gives me an absolute headache. I told him to go down to the hardware store—Miller's Hardware, over on 4th street—to get a replacement, but he insists they don't make that specific size anymore. Mr. Miller is such a nice man, though. He helped me find the exact right grit of sandpaper when I was refinishing that old rocking chair we found at the estate sale last spring. The one with the spindle back? It took me three entire weekends to get through all the old varnish. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, right up into your nose, even if you wear one of those paper masks. I had to sweep the garage floor twice a day just to keep it from tracking into the house... \n\nBut yes, to answer your question again, no. I don't see myself as overweight. I just see what needs to be tidied up.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you ever feel like you look bigger or more overweight than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Are you... are you asking me the same thing again? I feel like we're going in circles here, doctor. I really don't think I look bigger. I just see the parts that need to be fixed, like I said. \n\nIt's funny how things go in circles, though. The buffing machine at work, it goes in circles, and if you don't hold it just right, it leaves these terrible swirl marks on the linoleum. We use this special wax, comes in a big five-gallon bucket. Smells like lemons and chemicals. Reminds me of the lemonade stand my granddaughter, Lily, set up last summer. She made $14 and spent it all on those little plastic horses you get at the toy store. She has a whole collection of them now, lines them up on her windowsill. The windowsill that Arthur still hasn't fixed the draft on, mind you. Winter is coming and I just know we're going to have a terrible time keeping the heating bill down... \n\nBut yes, doctor. For the third time, no. I don't feel like I look overweight.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of how well you're doing in life is strongly connected to how you feel about your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like things are under control. But if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I feel like I've just let myself go completely. It's a terrible feeling. Like dropping a whole tray of glasses. \n\nWhich actually happened to me once, back when I was taking classes at the community college. I was waitressing at this little diner on Route 9 to pay for my textbooks. The textbooks were so expensive, even back then. I remember my biology book alone was almost fifty dollars. It had this glossy cover with a picture of a cell dividing. Mitosis, I think it's called? Anyway, I was carrying this huge tray of water glasses to a big table of truckers, and my foot caught the edge of a floor mat that hadn't been taped down right. The whole thing went crashing. Glass everywhere, water all over the linoleum. The manager, Mr. Henderson, he was furious. He had this little mustache that would twitch when he got mad. He made me sweep it all up, and then mop the whole dining room before I could go home. I didn't get out of there until almost two in the morning. And Arthur was waiting up for me, worried sick because this was before cell phones, of course. He had made a pot of coffee that was practically sludge by the time I got back...\n\nBut yes, if I don't look neat and small, I feel like everything is just a mess. Like I'm failing at it all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of self-worth is strongly connected to how you feel about your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: I feel like... didn't you just ask me that? Or something exactly like it? We were just talking about this, doctor. Yes, my body shape matters a lot to how I feel about myself. If I'm not keeping myself small, I just feel awful, like I'm not doing a good job at anything. \n\nIt's sort of like when I'm doing the floors at the office building. If I miss a spot, the whole hallway looks wrong to me. Even if nobody else notices, I know it's there. Just like that stain on the carpet in the lobby of the third floor. I've tried everything on it. Club soda, vinegar, that expensive foaming spray they buy us from the supply catalog. Nothing gets it out completely. It's from when someone dropped a cup of blackberry tea, I think. Blackberries are terrible for staining. My grandmother used to make blackberry preserves every August, and our fingers would be purple for a week. She had this huge copper kettle she'd boil them in on the stove. The kitchen would get so hot, just stifling, because she wouldn't open the windows. She said the draft would ruin the set of the jam. I still have some of her old mason jars down in the basement, the ones with the glass lids and the wire clamps... \n\nBut yes, to answer your question again, how I feel about my weight is basically how I feel about myself. If I'm not small, I'm just not doing well.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Doctor, I really... I feel like you're not listening to me. You asked me this exact same thing about the mirror and looking bigger just a few minutes ago. I don't see things larger than they are, I just see what needs to be fixed. I don't know how many different ways I can say it.

My sister Margaret, she's two years older than me, and she can barely get up the stairs in her house anymore because of her knees. She had that replacement surgery last fall, but the physical therapist said she didn't do her exercises right, so now she's worse off than before. I keep telling her she needs to walk, just around the block, but she says her neighborhood isn't safe for walking anymore. He's this young guy, just took over the practice from Dr. Miller who retired down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf Coast. Anyway, Dr. Aris has this huge saltwater fish tank in his waiting room, and I swear those fish look stressed. You can tell when a fish is stressed, their colors get all dull. I had a goldfish when I was a little girl, won him at the county fair by tossing a ping pong ball into a fishbowl. My mother was furious because she had to go buy a proper tank and a filter and all that. We named him Barnaby, and he lived for five years, which is practically a century for a fair goldfish...\n\nWhat did you ask me? Laxatives and things? No, no. I don't take any of those pills or anything. I just keep things tidy. Just keep moving, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself mainly based on your body weight or shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do, yes. It's just about being tidy, really. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like I'm keeping everything in order. It's like when I clean the floors at the middle school where I work. I've been there for fifteen years now. The kids are messy, of course, especially in the cafeteria. They drop those little milk cartons and they explode everywhere. And the smell of sour milk, oh, it's just impossible to get out of the linoleum if you don't mop it up right away. The principal, Mr. Henderson, he's a stickler for the floors. He always says, \"A clean floor is a happy school,\" which is a bit silly if you ask me, but he means well. He's retiring next year, actually. Going to spend more time with his grandkids in Ohio. I've never been to Ohio. I hear it's very flat... \n\nOh, right, judging myself. Yes, well, if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've let myself go. Like I'm not doing my job right, keeping myself neat. It just feels wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do your thoughts about your weight or shape interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or other important activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, of course. I'd never let my work slip. The buffing machine we use, it's this old industrial thing, heavy as a tractor. You really have to lean into it to keep it steady, otherwise it pulls you right across the hallway. The new guy, Tommy, he started last month and he nearly took out a row of lockers his first time using it. I told him, you have to guide it from your hips, not your shoulders. He's a nice kid, though. Reminds me of my nephew David. David's over in Seattle now, working for one of those computer companies. He sends us coffee beans sometimes, these fancy roasts that smell like blueberries. My husband, Arthur, he can't stand them. He just wants his Folgers from a can, like he's had for forty years...\n\nOh, you were asking about my daily life and things. Well, I do avoid family dinners now, and the potlucks at church. I just can't sit there with all those casseroles and heavy dishes. Arthur says I'm around everyone without really joining in anymore. But I just prefer to keep things tidy, you know? It's easier if I just stay out of the kitchen when everyone's eating.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel like you appear overweight or larger than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't think I look larger than I am. I just see what's there, you know? I just see where things aren't quite as tidy as they should be. It's like when you're looking at a window that hasn't been washed properly. You can see the streaks right away if the light hits it just right. We have these huge windows in the school library, facing east, so the morning sun just blasts right through them. If I don't use the squeegee exactly right, Mrs. Gable—she's the librarian—she'll point out every single smudge. She's got these thick glasses on a chain around her neck, and she'll peer over them at the glass. She's been reading this new mystery series, something about a baker who solves murders in a small town in Vermont. I tried reading one once, but I just couldn't get into it. I prefer biographies, real people, you know? I read a fascinating one about Eleanor Roosevelt last winter when we had that big snowstorm and the power was out for two days. Arthur had to get the generator running just so we wouldn't freeze... \n\nWhat was I saying about the mirror? Oh. No, I don't think I look overweight. I just see the parts that need work. The parts that aren't neat yet.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you feel like you appear overweight or larger than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I think we just... didn't I just say? Well, like I mentioned, no, I don't think I look bigger than I really am. I just see the parts that aren't quite right yet. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, though, Arthur has this terrible magnifying mirror in our bathroom for his shaving. Mr. Miller is such a nice man, though. He helped me find the exact right grit of sandpaper when I was refinishing that old rocking chair we found at the estate sale last spring.
